#4002 1 2005-08-11T19:30:57Z talkshowhost
I've noticed a lot of younger people in these forums confessing their love for God. I used to be like this in elementary school--a Catholic elementary school. I remember being in grade 6 and having this unbelievable love for the Christian church (I was incredibly religious). Why I was like that, I'm not sure. It's not like I made a choice to be that way; it was just part of my upbringing, and I went along with it, no questions asked...

Well, that's how it was for awhile. As I began to grow, my curiosity soared and I began asking a lot of questoins. I explored the Christian religion and the like, and realised how a lot of my beliefs were very, very different from the Christian beliefs.

I guess it's just weird reading someone saying that they love God and whatnot, and realising that I, too, was like that at some point in time. When I was that age, I never, ever thought I'd grow out of the religion I was raised with. (I would have said 'lost faith', but I think that would have implied something a little different.) But here I am! Crazy, that.

So, my question posed to the religious folk: why are you religious? Did you make a formal choice to continue with the way you've been brought up? Or were you not religious before, but made an effort to practice whatever you believe in?

And my question posed to all the non-religious folk: why aren't you religious? Was there a defining moment where you lost faith? Was it because something happened, or was it because you realised that the religion you were practicing wasn't up to snuff?

#90053 4 2005-08-11T22:19:28Z talkshowhost
Quote: Keron Cyst, 2005-08-11T19:46:28Z
EDIT What I don't see about atheists is that if they win (nihilism's true), they lose, and if they lose, they utterly lose (if you win, after you die you're permanently plunged into nothingness, which is frightening ACT since you can't feel anything even if you wanted to, not even pain, and definitely not joy; and if you lose, you go to a lonely lake of monstrous fire for all eternity) huh.gif Is a permanent end worth the temporary satisfaction (seconds, even, if you're talking about intercourse) of indulging in your sinful nature?

There was a famous mathematician and philosopher who used that same argument to show why you should believe in the Christian doctrine. He argued that it was like playing a game of chance. He said that if you assume that god and eternal life is infinity, and assume that nothing is, well, 0, then you can create some probability equation to show that if you win with atheism, you still lose. (For the life of me, I can't remember who it was.)

Quote: Keron Cyst, 2005-08-11T19:46:28Z
If I wasn't Christian I'd be really terrified about death and would want to really look into Christianity, and maybe other similar religions and see what they have to say about life & death. I mean, who cares if you "set your mark in this world" and people thousands of years after you know who you are? You're not around to enjoy it!

What is it about nothingness that terrifies you? (It was fear that banned zero for the longest time because people wouldn't stand to be told that nothingness actually existed--which stunted the growth of the mathematical realm.) If you didn't exist, you wouldn't have anything to really worry about. The way I see it is every good book needs an ending. Or, if you prefer, look at it this way: were you concerned with life before you existed? (ie. before you were born you didn't have a care in the world.)

I find that a lot of people flock to a form of religion because of fear--the fear of where we're going, what we're doing, and what happens next. No one really knows the answers, but if you pretend to know, people will follow. (Please note: I'm not trying to be insulting to anyone who believes in an organised religion.)

Another thing I dislike about organised religion is how they have a set of rules that cannot be adjusted to suit the times--they can only be changed to suit the people in charge and what they think is right. Just take 'bible thumpers' for example: they're dead set on trying to shove ancient laws down other people's throats (such as anti-homosexuality, prolife, etc etc). However, to do this, they're selecting a small, finite set of rules that come from the bible. If they believe in those, why not continue to believe daughters should be sold into slavery, and, well, any other ancient rule that falls under the catagory of WTF?

With regards to what I am: I'd have to say I'm an existentialist. I believe that life is completely absurd and meaningless--and it's not until you accept these little tidbits that you'll be truly happy.


I'm hungry.

#90080 6 2005-08-12T01:23:50Z brickman
I have never really been religious, although it seems my parents wanted me to be considering they're STILL forcing me to go to Sunday School. I mean, I'm gonna be a Junior in highschool this year, if I don't believe yet no ammount of reading stupid passages is going to make me. But anyways, my logic for not beleiving is this: if there was a perfect and all-powerful god out there, we would know it for fact by now. Not on faith, not from the pages of a thousand year old book, we would know it and see evidence of it as much as the fact that there is air. I mean, there would be no refuting it. In order for god to be leaving us to our own actions this much when we're doing this badly, he's got to be either non-existant or in no way an incarnation of all that is good, in which case he's obviously not the same god you're praying to and thus its not doing you much good. I've already stated the "too many conflicting religions" thing in previous threads, so I'll skip it.

As for the "everything nothing" arguement, there's two holes. First, you don't lose nothing if you turn out wrong, because if there's nothing after life than you're going to want back the large percentage of it that you spent praying and confessing and whatever once you realize its wasted. Second, the assumption that I "lose everything" assumes that my non-belief makes me let myself become a lying, cheating, stealing and generally mass-sinning jerk, which is almost as big of a logic leap as the ones you had to make to have faith. I'm still going to be a good person, I'm going to abide by the laws of society and the rules of morals and ethics, I'm going to give to charity--no intelligent diety is sending me to hell just because I didn't spend some time praying to someone who had given us no real reason to believe he existed. And many very religious christians are going to grow up to be enron executives or murderers or any number of other personalities that have no place being in heaven even if they spent one out of every ten waking hours of their life praying. The rules set out by religions, or at least the good ones, don't require you to have some crazy and unsupported faith to obey them. And if in fact the cutoff really is measured in the time spent in church instead of your actions outside of it, then I think I'm going to find better company in hell anyways. After all, that crazy diety wouldn't be living down there, and anyone that crazy would be bad enough to ruin paradise singlehandedly. Trust me, god is not that petty. If he was, there would be physical evidence biting us on the nose every time we stepped outside.

#90097 8 2005-08-12T04:02:51Z maximo
Why am I religious? That's a deep question and it'll take some time to explain, so please, bear with me.

First off, I'm 16 (I'm not really a youngun anymore, at least by these board's standards) and I've been a babtized believer for just over a year now. I have however known who the Christian God was my whole life. I was raised at first Catholic (for like 3 years) then Methodist (for 11). This doesn't mean I was abiding by these rules, just that I was labeled these things. When I was wee little, God was my super hero. He could fly and cast powerful spells and help me when I scraped my knee. That sorta blind faith a young kid raised in the Church has. However, at about age 12 I became analytical of my life and in particular my supposed faith. At my church no one had answers, at all. So slowly I talked myself out of church and God. I was first just a disbeliever and decided I didn't care enough to think about it anymore because I had already proved contrary. Then I started thinking again (I have oodles of free time and I just think and think) and I came to the short-lived conclusion that you can't possibly know for sure one way or the other (agnosticism). I mean, there was no apparent physical evidence for or against God because he was well, not of this universe supposedly. It was all fanciful and I rejected it. After a bit I came upon the belief of Deism and fell in line with it. I don't think science can or ever will be able to account for the creation of a universe (something I still believe) and Deism fit what I was looking for exactly. God made the place, hit play and is now watching us beat the crap out of each other laughing and sippin on a stoggie, probally toasting it up with Satan. Well you get the point, it was a joke to think God was still around, you couldn't see or feel or hear Him, just dumb to think He could do anything for me, so why live for him if He didn't care. Well this lasted for about 2 years and I was all "screw the world, it's going to Hell anways" with everyone I met and talked to. If it wasn't for numero uno, then it wasn't gonna get done, period. I mean I still was a good kid by today's standards, never once drank, smoke, partied, etc. (I'm not the social butterfly type). Well this sorta "whatever whatever" attitude led me into some pretty serious addictions (if you care to know what, PM me. I'll tell anyone who asks, but I don't do it in a public setting) and they pretty much owned my soul. No joke, Satan pwnd me. I no longer had control over my thoughts, time, actions, body. I mean I really was being led along like a blind, mute, deaf, quadraplegic by my personal longings and desires. I kept my habits secret and no one ever really found out... ever (until the aftermath when I told the people affected). Well this went on in increasing severity for about 5 months. What a freaking enjoyable time. I will not lie, what I was doing was really, really fun and easy to get. Well after a while I crossed some lines that I swore I would never cross, and it opened my eyes to the fact that I wasn't in control of my life anymore (again, this can be explained only in PM). I tried for about a month to stop on my own accord. No way I tell anyone, no way I seek help from anyone. This was my beef with me, and I was big enough to handle it. I wasn't, by any means. It only got worse for me, despite all my prolonged efforts. Pretty much I sucked at life for that month or so. Well, one night I was like... well man, this is stupid but I can't do it. Trust me, admitting defeat is not something someone like me does lightly. I came to the conclusion that asking God for help could do no possible harm, and could either do nothing, or good. What's to lose, right? I prayed the dumbest prayer ever. Pretty much I was mocking God while I asked for His help with my life. Well, quite to my surprise, slowly I saw a change. I could start saying "no" to myself, start walking away from what I wanted but knew couldn't have because it hurt other people. It was like the most radical change ever. Now your probably expecting me to say, "Well, that proved it, and I ran down to church jumped in a pool and was saved," but that's not how it went. It really only cracked the door open for me. It put the questions back in my head about whether or not God cared. I had to know man, I just had to. I started searching. Not really an aimed search, just looking for what was out there, strictly scientific. I analyzed and thought about everything I came across for a long time. I read a lot of books about what facts there were for the Christian faith, both for and against and to my surprise (and honestly to a little dismay) I found the evidence for God so much more convincing. I really don't feel like going into all I found because that is a whole 'nother conversation altogether. Suffice to say, I found a lot more convincing evidence (archaeological, scientific (physics and biology mostly), and a little other people (mostly 2 main players, one who is Agnostic, the other Christian). I used a lot of people as bouncing boards for what I found, and a lot of athiests really didn't have answers to some facts I was finding and telling them. My biology teacher who studied evolution pretty much fell on his face trying to defend his position (which I know, isn't a great task to topple a high school biology teacher, but the example remains). Well, that summer I went to a church conference called CiY (Christ in Youth). The auditorium was packed with about 3000 (I think that's the number, suffice to say it was a very large number) teens from all over the Midwest. During worship watching people around me who I figured people who were hopeless cases, or the other extreme already "right with God" crying, screaming, dancing, shouting, jumping, laughing, hugging, praying, etc. I could not help but think that maybe this evidence was deeper than some manuscripts found in a desert. I saw for the first time in my life God moving in a place. I mean, you really couldn't deny it sitting in that auditorium. People of all backgrounds and stories helpless to the power in this place. I mean, nothing shuts up 3000 teens like prayer. I mean even the rudest, most obnoxious people shut up for prayer in that place. They recognized something was there; whether or not they wanted it was another thing, but they knew they needed to shut up for probably the first time in their lives. I was one of those people. God didn't need silence because God didn't care. At least that is what I thought going into this conference. That soon changed. I couldn't help but feel the force in this place moving and working. Any thoughts of agnosticism were pretty much chrushed there, and really Deism was out the window. I was baptized at that conference and since have been passionately in love with God. I have slipped plenty of times (ugh...) and tried really hard to go back to my old ways, but God's love is too strongly revealed to me. My faith is built upon this foundation of fact and decorated with faith. I regularly pick up a discovery magazine and read what the new finds are to check my facts against the most recent finds. I still read and research my Faith extensivly. I check bible verses against the original Greek texts for accuracy, I check verses I don't understand with 2 different commentaries on the Bible and my youth minister (who is probally the most analytical guy I know other than me) and he *usually* has the answer. The times he doesn't I go to other sources (Internet, other teachers, magazines, etc.) It's a pretty thourough checking system to ensure what I put so much time to is what I should be doing. Right now is the best day of my life. It's not because I did anything cool or something cool happened to me. It's because today I got to hang out with the God of the universe, who loves me more than all of you put together could ever with all the time of this world ten times over. I mean, all the dumb stuff I did during those 5 plus months before I was baptized has been wiped away by the sacrifice God made because he loved me more than I can ever know. Every day is the best day of my life because of this love. Nothing I have ever tried or searched for has been better or more solidly built. I love God so much I couldn't tell you in a hundred posts this long. His love is all that matters to me. God isn't number one on my priorities list, He *is* the list. I can't really say much more. That's my story, start to finish. If you want to know the details and whatnot PM me (any of you) and I'll give ya the personal stuff.

You asked, I answered. Sorry for any typos, misspellings, or stupid grammar errors in there; I'm not the best speller or typist.
#90100 9 2005-08-12T04:38:44Z Lucidium
About that winning / losing argument, here's a interesting question:

What if, after you die, you get resurrected by some all powerful aliens who have been watching over us. These aliens think that religions are nothing but the childish ideas of a young race, and prefer rather to trust only in science. As such, anyone who has avoided the snares of religion is rewarded with eternal life, and anyone who believed in a religion is punished until they have atoned for their gullibility.

This turns the argument around, and points out it's main flaw. It assumes the religion is correct in the first place.

I think I may have stated my explanation poorly. What I meant was that if the main requirement for getting into heaven was how religious you were, as many bad people would be there as good. And there would be at least as many good people in hell as bad, because most of the bads actually take shelter behind religion so that they can convince themselves that their evil deeds will never catch up with them. I personally cannot imagine, however, a god who would set up this rule. Why? Because making how strongly you believed in him more or equally important to your actions is a sign of what would in humans be called extreme pettiness, although you can call it whatever you want in a diety since he's above our adjectives. However, the point is that if he cared that much about you believing in him, then I'm 100% sure he would have solid physical proof of his existence for us. He wouldn't require a leap of faith, because there's currently no reason for us to believe in him except a book that in itself is the only thing saying its authentic. He would make sure there's a reason for even the most skeptical among us to believe that he exists. After all, if I don't know you exist its not my fault I'm not worshipping you. The alternative is supposed to be siding with god, who represents good, or siding with satan, who represents evil. Its not supposed to be choosing between a huge leap of faith and belief in something you don't know exists vs things you can actually proove exist. Anyone who thinks belief or non-belief is going to make the difference between getting in or not is completely missing the point.

#90423 39 2005-08-13T03:59:31Z Atilla
I am agnostic, for a number of reasons.

Firstly, because it's impossible to ever tell. When someone is struck by lightning, the religious say "He must have been a foul sinner. God struck him down for his evil." The atheists then reply "No, it was because there was an accumulation of charge in the clouds and he happened to be standing on a mountain holding a copper spike in the air." To which the religious people reply, "Ah, but God caused the accumulation of charge." After this, the conversation degenerates to the equivalent of people screaming "IS NOT!" and "IS TOO!" at each other.

Secondly, because there are thousands of religions out there, and most contradict not only each other, but themselves. The probablilty that the one you believe in, or indeed any of them at all, is right is next to nothing.

I believe most of the self-contradictory elements in a religion are caused by sociological influences. Take Christianity. God is supposed infinitely merciful, yet condemns the unrighteous to eternal suffering. Surely a program of rehabilitation would be more appropriate. And what's the point of telling people about heaven and hell? Even criminals can be bribed and blackmailed. They are, however, very useful for keeping the plebs under the influence of the Church. I find the fact that the first commandment is to worship no other god rather suspicious, too. "I may be a murderer, but at least I'm not heathen scum". And if you look at the history of Europe, you will find the word of the Church is more powerful than that of God. Do the Crusades, Inquisition and burnings sound like justifiable acts in religion that advocates loving thy neighbour and commands followers that "Thou Shalt Not Kill"? It's not an ad. You can't have small print saying unbelievers, heretics, criminals, prostitutes, and old women who wouldn't be so weird if their kids visited once in a while are excluded.

In fact, I think justification of any act by saying "God says..." is ridiculous. You can say that about anything. 'It happened, so clearly God meant it to happen. He is omnipotent, and could have stopped it if He wished.' Furthermore, many religions say no mortal can know the mind of god. They then tell us what he wants. Notice a discrepancy?

That said, it isn't impossible for there to be an element life beyond the physical. Perhaps a kind of god exists through the belief of humans - things are regularly done because 'God wills it', whether or not that god actually exists. Defining what exists and what doesn't is difficult in any event - money, for example, is just peices of plastic. In fact, in this high-tech age, it's more often just magnetic fluctions or patterns of light (better known as numbers). However, it's still incredibly powerful. Something doesn't neccesarily need physical form to exist - humanity practically IS things with no existance in time and space. And time and space are limited. What we like to think of as reality may just be a simulation, like the Matrix. Our universe may be an experiment in quantum mechanics by aliens. There may be endless ranks of universes, one for every possible sequence of events, so that nothing is real - there is merely the probablity of reality.

That said, we need to realise that we are just a clever animal with limited perception. Where everyone really lives is in a small universe which orbits, quite conveniently, around the inside of our heads. Everything we percieve is tainted by own perceptions and prejudices. Attempting to apply a human reason to things like the creation of the universe is foolish. A god may have created it because that is a function of godhood - just as being inquisitive is a function of our humanity. In one sense, the universe was never created - it has existed since the beginning of time and will until the end of time. It must have - what is time without a univirse for it to elapse in?

Anyway, that's my two cents (although it's probably more like 2 dollars).

By the way, has anyone read Strata or Small Gods, by Terry Pratchett? If not, I reccomend them (and all his other books for that matter).

All Christians want is it mentioned objectively. It is a very viable theory just as evolution is a viable theory. If all we teach in schools is evolution, then 1000's of kids grow up oblivious to the other ideas out there, and as such any work they will do in the field will be skewed, and their answers skewed respectfully. I'm not asking for it to be taught as fact. But evolution is commonly taught as the *only* theory in schools (I can testify, my biology teacher taught it as fact and not theory) and that's not the case. Most kids my age aren't aware enough to look into anything else like I have, and as such they grow up thinking the Creationism is some 18th-century jargon that was shot down years ago. In fact, recent years have shown an increase in scientists who at least acknowledge the "miracle" of life which show signs of intelligent design. Most don't go as far as to subscribe to Creationism, but they admit that their findings are pointing towards an amount of order and planning that is hard for natural processes to account for. In summary, teach both sides as theory and I'm fine. You don't even have to mention Christianity. @maximo

I'm not denouncing the entire Christian religion because of the acts of the church. I'm saying that the OFFICIAL version is seriously flawed. Most Christians (at least in Australia) now adopt what I like to call Pick-And-Mix Christianity, whereby one ignores those parts of the religion which were, perhaps, generated by corruption rather than faith. I went to a Catholic school. I have no problem with Christians. I just think that listening to official dogma is not the way to enlightenment - wisdom cannot be taught, and must come from within. This is why discussion like this is useful - it helps people analyse their own beliefs and their validity.

On your comment that the fence isn't wide enough to sit on, I assure you it is. For you there may be no difference between agnosticism and atheism, since both refuse to believe in any kind of gods. However, atheists not only shun belief, but declare that is most certainly not, never was, and never can be any aspect to life beyond the physical. As an agnostic, I believe that there may or may not be some supernatural force present in the world, but since its nature and/or existance has not yet been described to my satisfaction, there is no point engaging in vague rituals and belief systems in its honour. It's the difference between voting against something, or refusing to vote at all, if that helps.

edit in @ Eli

How is it stupid to research for hours, reading books on both sides of the case, looking online for the answer? And when the evidence is stronger on one side than the other, making a decision for the stronger side? What's stupid is generalizing Christians as stupid becuase they have faith in some degree. An old argument: when you sit in a chair, you cannot know for certain whether or not it will hold you. I mean, you could try *not* sitting on it and say there is no way to know, but then you don't get to sit down. You could say "I won't sit on it because it can be proven that it won't fall when I sit on it." But you sit, most of the time without thinking about it. #91197 67 2005-08-15T05:06:07Z maximo
Talkshowhost... I've extensively researched the factuallity and reliabilty of the Bible because it is the basis of all Christian Faith. I'll try quickly to summarize the results of my findings. This will in no way be comprehensive and the numbers are heavily rounded from memory (I'm not good with specifics, but the point will still be clear I hope).

First off, I have a Greek-to-English interliterary Bible to check the original Greek against our translations.

The verse I tried to reference earlier is Romans 1:27 which reads

Quote: Romans 1:27 (NLV)
and in the same way also the men, giving up natural intercourse with women, were consumed with passion for one another. Men commited shameless acts with men and received in their own persons the due penalty for their error.

And the Greek word for word translation (this is in no way a complete English sentence; it's the Greek broken down by word and translated)

Quote
likewise indeed also the males having sent off the natural use of the female were burned thoroughly in the lust of them to one another, males in males the shamelessness working thoroughly and the return wage which it is necessary of the deceit of them in themselves reveiving back.

The phrase "males in males" uses the same word both times, so it clearly doesn't mean males and boys. In the preceding verse Paul says this, which isn't the topic, but applies none the less.

Quote: Rom 1:26 (NLV)
For this reason God gave them up to degrading passions. Their women exchanged natural intercourse for unnatural.

The Greek is too indecipherable to be of any use for 1:26. This verse clearly points out that males and males having intercourse is unnatural. And seeing as they were penalized for this, we can reach the conclusion it is a sin before God.

---

That pertained only to the topic at hand; now onto the validity of the Bible as a whole.

---

There are 2 known manuscripts telling of Hanibal crossing the Alps (or prynesses, I never can remember which one; it's insignicant. He crossed some mountains, as told in these accounts). Both are dated 100's of years after the event occurred which means they were passed down orally (most likely) and then written down later for record keeping. 2 copies. Both are widely different than each other in terms of minor details and extra facts. However, it is common knowledge and an indisputable fact that Hanibal did in fact cross these mountains. This is based solely on 2 accounts both 100's of years after the actual events happened.

There are around 5000 manuscripts of the New Testament. The earliest are dated even *conservatively* to about 50 years after the death of Christ (that is very conservative, mind you; recent reports date them as early as 2-30) which in archaeological terms a blink of an eye. And most manuscripts fall within two centuries of this time period. This is like a second to archaeologists. There is not enough time for them to be blown out of proportion because eyewitnesses still lived and would have spoke out against the lies. This is especially true because the Jews and the Romans were strongly opposed to this outburst of revolution. Had these claims been outlandish and untrue, they would have said so. They had the authority and people's favor to do these things and people would have listened to them because they were reputable eyewitnesses to the events being told in scripture. 5000 manuscripts, the earliest within 50 years of the events. This doesn't include the fact that there are only .5% words in dispute today in the entire New Testament. That's about 200 words, and none of them change the facts. Most are simple words like how many people were there at this time or what time of day it was, etc. None of them change or alter the facts recorded in the Bible. So nearly identical, the most manuscripts for any historical piece ever, astoundingly close time period to actual events, what other conclusion is there? Also, every translation you read today is done from the Greek texts. They don't take the NIV and translate it to the NLV. They take the Greek and translate it to all the versions you have today. It's all from source, and not from "well these guys said this and they said that, we like this guy more so well use his."

---

That is excessively summarized. Thislinky dink is a more organized version of my arguments and pretty much hits most of the same points I do, which is weird because I found that after I wrote all of that above. So to believe in what I read as true isn't a stretch at all. I check all I read against the original Greek to ensure the translation I'm reading is accurate; I have 2 different commentaries I check against each other for accuracy. What I read is what they wrote.

Yes, I think we all know what I think about the validity of any official religious publication by now. I point out that a lot of the Bible could be explained by a slight exaggeration of facts. To whit, my non-theistic account of Jesus' life:

About 2000 years ago, a bloke called Jesus was born. A devout Jew, great philosopher and charismatic leader, he devoted his life to helping the sick and downtrodden, perhaps not unlike an ancient Mother Teresa. Because his efforts were inspired by his religion, he often told people about his faith, including the love of God the Father. After stories of his exploits spread through rumour, this was misinterpreted, and he became known as the Son of God. Stories of his deeds also became exaggerated, and healing the sick with bandages and herbs became healing with a mere touch. His friends and relatives protested, of course, but the stories spread too quickly and their voices were lost in the clamour of eager converts. He gained too much influence from his reputation, however, and was eventually put to death because the authorities feared him.

I also think some section of the Bible (Revelations) may have been inspired by the consumption of strange mushrooms. Uh, not meaning to be disrespectful or anything. And possibly some parts were edited by corrupt officials. I have heard there is evidence that the Bible was tampered with to reduce womens' power, but since I've never seen any of it I dismiss this as feminist propaganda.

Furthermore, the fact that no-one can work out how the hell you spell 'Koran' (or 'Quo'ran' or 'Quran' or whatever today's flavour is) throws doubt on exactly how accurate a document can be after it's been translated through multiple languages.

And yes, maximo, I know you disagree

#91685 110 2005-08-16T20:00:23Z Lucidium
I'd just like to make what I consider a very important point about evidence.

A lot of evidence cited as backing up faith-based beliefs isn't really evidence. It is found after the belief has been established, and is called evidence because it agrees with the previously decided upon details.

Science, when done properly*, takes all of the available evidence, and then suggests an hypothesis. Using this hypothesis, predictions are made based on it, and if they agree with new evidence, the hypothesis is accepted as a theory.

You can find evidence for pretty much anything, like the decline in pirates causing global warming, or that old joke about a bear/tiger/scary creature repellent. Just because there's some observations to support it, doesn't make it true.

Related to this point is another difference between faith and science. Science is not around for the sole purpose of finding the truth about the world. It's around because it's useful. You can predict things with it, and you can invent and create and progress with it.

This is one of the main reasons I've decided to be an atheist.


* I say "when done properly", because there's plenty of "scientists" out there who will prejudice and skew their results for whatever reason.

On preview - Got a bit carried away with the bolding there. But hey, I think it's important.

#91739 112 2005-08-16T23:00:43Z Lucidium
Quote: maximo, Aug 16 2005, 08:16 PM
this goes both ways you know.  There are plenty of scientists out there not taking the facts but as you said, tryin to find facts to support their side.  That's not science as you said.  There really is no way to do complete objective research.
I'm not sure why you said "this goes both ways". The whole thing about some scientists not doing objective research is exactly the point I was making in that footnote. I wasn't making a sly comment about people supporting religion with science, if that's what you thought. I only put "scientists" in quote marks to show my disdain for the kind of people that cheat like this.
I said that because I wanted to concede in advance that the world of science isn't perfect, because the fact that scientists do this too is a valid argument.

Hopefully though, most cases of this get caught by other scientists, and one of the most important requirements for credibility in the scientific world is to be published in a peer reviewed journal. If you're screwing about with your data, you won't get published.

Anyway, onto your point about prophecies. I've got to admit that it's surprising evidence, but the scientist (read: pedantic nitpicker) in me has a couple questions. Like:
How can we be sure the prophecies were made before the event, instead of just written about afterwards?
And, how can we know that there weren't hundreds of incorrect prophecies made, that just didn't get passed down through history because they weren't considered amazing or important (precisely because of the fact that they weren't correct)?

Another important qualifier for evidence is that it is repeatable, because if it isn't, there's a statistically tiny likelihood of it being real. For example, there's plenty of papers out there with apparent miracle inventions, but because no one else has managed to recreate the experiments, they don't get published.
It's like sampling a population of people from some town. You could look at one person, and conclude that everyone in the town has black hair, brown eyes, and really likes waffles. This would be correct if the town only contained one person. However, as there are actually hundreds of thousands in most towns, you would have to repeat the experiment on at least a few hundreds of people before you really knew what the town was like.
In terms of the prophecies, I'd want to know if any other prophecies were made that turned out wrong. Because of the distance in time, the only "samples" we're looking at are the prophecies that were right. We can't know if these were the only prophecies made.

As an aside, I'm arguing about these underlying, less personal issues for two reasons. Partly because I want to make it clear why I place such trust in the methods and requirements of science. And partly because arguing about personal beliefs can rarely be more than stating your own opinion in angrier and angrier terms.

#91744 113 2005-08-16T23:10:16Z nevermore
I'm not quite sure I understand your "repeatable" argument. Could you explain it in a biit more depth?
#91746 114 2005-08-16T23:12:08Z Lucidium
Which part? The bit about not trusting experiments until someone else has done them, or the bit about the guy who likes waffles?

[edit] Eh, I'll just expand on both.

About the repeatability of experiments.
Anyone can write a paper (as in, scientific essay detailing their experiment and conclusions) on an experiment they claim to have done. Like, you could say you've invented an infinite power source by taping a cat to a piece of buttered toast. But if no one else can recreate your cat-toast-engine, why should anyone believe you?

About the sampling thing.
Say you had a bag of 50 coloured balls. You don't know what colours there are, or even if there's more than one colour, but you are allowed to take a ball out to look at it.
If you take one ball out, and it's blue, would you then conclude that all 50 balls are blue? It's still very possible that there could be different colours.
If you took ten balls out, and they were all blue, it would be much less possible for there to be different colours.
Obviously, if you poured out all 50, and they were all blue, it would be impossible for there to be any other colours.

The more times you repeat the experiment of looking at a ball, the more you can narrow down the possibilities.

[/edit]

#91871 124 2005-08-17T08:07:37Z Atilla
On prophecies:

The big problem with such things is what is known as selective reporting. For example, it is a very common misconception that sharks are horrifyingly dangerous man-eaters, because of the sensational media reports every time someone is injured by one. In fact, sharks don't really like the taste of humans (especially ones wearing synthetic wetsuits). Think about it. Some surfer gets his leg bitten off by a 'man-eater' and survives. Do you really think a one-legged, badly bleeding human could outswim a creature designed by four billion years of evolution (or by a deity, if you're so inclined) to kill swimming things? No, they survive because the shark doesn't WANT to eat them. Uh, where was I? Oh yes. You never see "SHOCK! SWIMMER FAILS TO BE EATEN BY SHARK!" in the newspapers, even though (or rather because) it happens so frequently. Coconuts, on the other hand, are regarded as benign despite the fact that they kill more people. My point is that it's very hard to judge how many prophecies someone failed to fulfill after 2000 years, because everyone's been standing around going "Wow! That guy Jesus fulfilled another prophecy!" for the last two millenia. So we have no sure way of knowing just how well he did fit such prophecies as there were.

And furthermore, on the spaghetti appendage thing:

In a spirit of equality, I have devised two scientific hypotheses that can explain any apparently 'supernatural' phenomena.

THE COMPLICATED ONE

At the quantum level, it is theorised that particles do not have a single position in time and space; rather, they exist 'smeared' across a range of possible positions. Extrapolating from this, I theorise that our universe is just one of infinite ranks of universes, one for every possible sequence of events. Some differ from our own by the orbit of a single electron; others are not even recognisable as the same universe. These infinite worlds, which lie a shadows' thickness from our own, may interfere with each other in places where the fabric of reality is stretched. This is common at a quantum level because the huge forces concentrated in such a tiny area stretch the fabric of time and space as thin as a very thin thing indeed (Yes! Pratchett references for all!). However, interference may still occur on a macro scale; ghosts, for example, appear when photons get confused and think they're in a universe where the deceased still lives. QED.


THE SIMPLE ONE

Humans are opportunistic, dishonest, and easily decieved.


And, maximo, I said you would disagree with me because I knew you would, not to avoid a post.

#92285 141 2005-08-18T22:19:37Z iangb
Quote: maximo, 2005-08-18T21:14:34Z
If I have infinite marbles and give you half of them; infinity/2=infinity
Your maths isn't quite right. Infinity is not a number. It is a concept that can be used in maths, but not in the same way that normal numbers can (like imaginary number i). So for the 'infinity' numbers system, it is completely possible for half of infinity to equal infinity, because you cannot treat infinity as a normal number.

roughly, infinity * x = infinity, unless x = 0, in which case infinity * 0 = 0. As division is simply multiplying by 1/x the same is true for that. Adding anything other than -infinity to infinity = infinity, but infinity - infinity = 0.
You cannot have half an infinite number of marbles.

If there is a finite chance something will happen then in an infinite universe it is guarenteed to happen. There is no such thing as an infinite chance - if something is non-zero then it is finite, no matter how small it is (for a chance ranging from 0 to 1).

The real question therefore is - is an event physically possible. If it is, then there is a finite - maybe very very small, but still finite - chance that it will happen, so in an infinte universe it is guaranteed to happen. If it is not physically possible then the chance of it happening is 0 - and so it will never happen. Ever. Not in this universe, anyway.

I don't really have a point to this post, but it explains some stuff at least.

In an infinite universe there would be infinite copies of this universe, along with infinite copies of all the infinite possibilites of universes out there. I used to go to an American Baptist church (northern US, not to be confused with a Southern Baptist church). Anyway, I hated it. There were no kids my age, the music (and most of the services) were boring. When we moved a few years ago, we went to a Presbyterian church (which was even worse than the Baptist!). When we moved to our present location, we spent a long time trying to find a church. We thought we had found our church, but then we decided it was just not for us. So we resumed looking. We finally found our match in a Methodist church. I went through the Confirmation process, and thus joined the church. Ever since, I have gone on ~5 trips with the youth group, as well as meetings on Sundays and Wednesdays.

When we first started going to our present church, I was nervous about the 'tempo' of the services. However, we started going to the contemporary service, and that was probably one of the best things my family ever did. The music is especially good. We have a fantastic Praise Band, and spend more than 1/2 the service on music. The music we all is very upbeat.

So back to my original point: I feel that you can see religion as a very solemn, grave experience. Or, you can focus on the lighter points of it.

I haven't had time to read this whole thread, but I did see a comment earlier (I forget by whom) about good people getting into heaven. I would like to quote John 14:6. "I am the way, the truth, and the life. No one can come to the Father except through me." The way I personally interpert(sp?) this verse is that you can live a perfect life, but unless you have accepted Jesus as your savior, you cannot get into heaven. Forgive my 'preachy'-ness there.

Well, that's my stand on the issue. 2129 Carachters, in case you were curious.

#92383 147 2005-08-19T05:19:11Z stepself
Quote: iangb, Aug 18 2005, 05:19 PM
Quote: maximo, 2005-08-18T21:14:34Z
If I have infinite marbles and give you half of them; infinity/2=infinity
Your maths isn't quite right. Infinity is not a number. It is a concept that can be used in maths, but not in the same way that normal numbers can (like imaginary number i). So for the 'infinity' numbers system, it is completely possible for half of infinity to equal infinity, because you cannot treat infinity as a normal number.

roughly, infinity * x = infinity, unless x = 0, in which case infinity * 0 = 0. As division is simply multiplying by 1/x the same is true for that. Adding anything other than -infinity to infinity = infinity, but infinity - infinity = 0.
You cannot have half an infinite number of marbles.

For the sake of accuracy: "infinity - infinity = 0" is not necessarily true. In practical application, it rarely is. As you said, inifinty is not a number, so we can't treat it as such. We can look at how numbers tend as independent variables increase without bound. Consider this example: y=x/2 and y=x/3. For large values of x, both functions tend toward infinity. But the former tends toward infinity faster than the latter. For a large value of x (essentially infinite, let's say), y=x/2 will still be larger than y=x/3. Their difference will be a non-zero number. Thus, infinity - infinity != 0

This really has little to do with anything, but I find it fascinating nonetheless.

In response to ethics -
Being happy is a good thing by definition. Feeling happy literally means feeling good, and people enjoy feeling happy. One might say that happiness is a luxury.
Doing what our society may call a 'bad' thing will make at least one person unhappy. You are robbing someone of their luxury, taking away the fact that they feel good.
Robbing someone of happiness is a bad thing. Letting them keep it is a good thing. It's as simple as that. The ethics can't change, because humans don't enjoy feeling unhappy, unless they're emotionally masochistic. And I'm not even sure people like that exist.

#100744 216 2005-09-15T11:38:53Z Atilla
@macaddict: Evolution is not a myth. We can see it in action.

In northern America, there is a certain species of moth. This moth is usually a light grey shade, which blends in with the bark of the trees it lives on. There are occaisional throwbacks with dark wings. These get eaten fairly quickly by birds, since they are easier to see. On occasion, however, there is a forest fire. The grey trees are blackened, and now it is the light moths that stand out. They are nearly all eaten - the overwhelming majority of the remaining population is dark. And so is the next generation, because the colour of a moth's wings is a genetic trait. And so is the next generation, except for some throwbacks. However, by this time, the trees are recovering from the fire and becoming light again: the dark moths are gradually picked off and, within a few generations, nearly all the moths are light again. This has been recorded. We know evolution occurs because we can go and look.

Secondly: We know how old fossils are not only because they lie in a certain strata, but also through the use of advanced techniques such as carbon dating.

Thirdly: Believing someone cannot have any morals unless they agree with you is incredibly foolish and arrogant. It is this sort of behaviour which leads to atrocities like the Spanish Inquisition, the Crusades, and, indeed, September 11. Read my sig, dammit!

Fourth: You still haven't explained why your religion is better than any of the others. You're outnumbered, so you'd better have a good reason.

Fith: How do you know it was the hand of a Christian god that supposedly wrote this "moral code", and not the Noodly Appendage of the Flying Spaghetti Monster? Or the hand of Ganesh, God of Compassion?

Sixth: It is entirely possible that we percieve actions as right or wrong because it is an evolutionary advantage - it makes us cooperate. After all, humans aren't particularly strong, or fast. We don't have big claws and teeth, we don't have armour, we can't fly, we have a relatively poor sense of smell and hearing, and our vital organs are in a somewhat exposed position. Therefore, we have to work together to survive. And, of course, any organism's greatest competition is other members of its species, since they want exactly the same resources.

#101137 237 2005-09-17T05:44:06Z Atilla
Up with the Daily Llama!

Macaddict and JFlash: You have to chose a religion based on your own judgement. What makes that judgement superior to the moral judgements of an atheist?

And saying "because God says they're superior" or somesuch is not a valid answer. That is effectively saying "I am right because I am correct". The point is that at some stage you HAVE to make a decision as an individual.

Carbon dating is accurate up to 50 000 years not 6000. The HALF LIFE of C14 is just under 6000 years. Other radioactive isotopes, such as Uranium 235, can be used to date older objects. We can also analyse the magnetic properties of rocks, which depend on the period the stone set in because Earth's magnetic field changes over time.

@jflash: The Bible isn't really an unchanging standard. Firstly, it is a derivation of the Jewish holy book, which makes up the Old Testament. Secondly, the Islamic Koran is also derived from the Jewish holy texts, states that Jesus was a prophet (although not THE prophet) , and yet is somewhat different from the Bible. Furthermore, it is not an unchanging standard because even Christians can't agree on how to interpret it, and that interpretation changes over time - hence all the sects. If you were a Christian four hundred years ago, you would be trying to burn us at the stake for heresy even as I speak. I also point out that some Christians believe in evolution while others think everyone who proposes it should be summarily executed. The Bible says "Thou Shalt Not Kill". Does it specify that it excludes plants or non-human animals? No. Stop eating that innocent wheat, you murderers! Does it allow for self-defence, or the killing of condemned criminals? No. But the Catholic Church supports all these. In any event, as I undersand it, Christ overrode the rigid Ten Commandments with "Do unto others as you would have them do unto you" and "Love thy neighbour as thyself", both of which are open to interpretation and change.
In any event, an unchanging standard is not neccesarily required to make ethical decisions; you think it is because that is part of your belief system. For example, it's wrong to steal - but if your family is dying of starvation because the tyrannical government treats you as a slave, is it still wrong to steal bread from some rich buerocrat's table to ease their suffering? If you have an absolute and inflexible moral code, you have to say "No, stealing is wrong and can never be justified. Watching your family whither and die is a far more compassionate thing to do."

@macaddict: Buddhist do not have an unchanging standard. Buddhism encourages free thought and a questioning attitude, which is one reason I like it. There are many different forms of Buddhism which each have different codes of behaviour.

Furthermore, there are about ten million religions that have existed for as long or longer than yours. There are also many religions with "good rules", and atheists and agnostics are quite capable of devising "good rules" by themselves. I also point out that atheism and agnosticism have existed since the dawn of humanity.

And why is FSMism stupid? Hindus believe in gods with eight arms and the head of an elephant, and nobody claims that's crazy.

Atheism can explain a universal system of ethics; it is a function of our evolution. Humans are not particularly strong, or fast. We don't have an armoured hide, we don't have proper claws, we can't fly, our senses of hearing and smell are poor, and our vital organs are in an exposed position. We therefore have to cooperate to survive, and natural selection favours those who don't go around slaughtering their own species indiscriminantly. Many other animals (which Christianity declares have no souls) are also reluctant to kill their own species - often much more reluctant than humans.

In addition, if you believe that not a single scrap of the scientific evidence for evolution has any shred of validity, then it's clear that you are never going to accept anything short of rewinding the universe and playing it through again as evidence. Likewise for believing there is no evidence anywhere that could cast even the slightest doubt on Christianity. I guess it's true what they say - "mere facts cannot sway a true believer".

@Keron and jflash: Saying that non-religious peoples' ideas of ethics are driven entirely by the media and society is foolish, considering that (in Australia and America, at least) Christians are both more numerous and more vocal about their beliefs.

...

Have you ever seen such a huge rant? I think I'll go write a thesis on divine metaphysics.

#101736 252 2005-09-18T16:55:55Z Teedle
Quote: Keron Cyst, Sep 18 2005, 03:55 PM
@The Daily Llama: how on earth can there be a mutation so huge? If species can break away like that why haven't we seen extremely fast-living (living, as in their lifespans are really short) mutating and breaking off into other species in weeks?

Huh? It's not a mutation. It's a variety of small, subtle mutations that makes the colonies seperate and- eventually- become considered a seperate species. Once again, it's not A MUTATION; it's many, many small mutations that end up beneficial for the species. And it takes a very, very long time.

#101932 253 2005-09-18T21:47:53Z Lucidium
Quote: Keron Cyst, Sep 18 2005, 03:55 PM
If species can break away like that why haven't we seen extremely fast-living (living, as in their lifespans are really short) mutating and breaking off into other species in weeks?

We have. Plenty of times.
We've been doing it to plants for nearly a century, through hybridization and polyplodization, with plants like ferns, hemp nettles, and radish-cabbage hybrids. It's been observed in the Tragopogon genus, producing the mirus and miscellus species. Oenothera lamarckiana has been observed to produce a variant with a different number of chromosomes, that could not breed with the parent species. Speciation has been observed in Nereis acuminata, a worm commonly used in toxicology experiments. It even appears to have been seen in mice introduced to islands by humans .


(Owenby, M. 1950. Natural hybridization and amphiploidy in the genus Tragopogon. Am. J. Bot. 37:487-499.)
(de Vries, H. 1905. Species and varieties, their origin by mutation.)
(Weinberg, J. R., V. R. Starczak and P. Jora. 1992. Evidence for rapid speciation following a founder event in the laboratory. Evolution. 46:1214-1220.)
(Britton-Davidian, J., J. Catalan, et al. (2000) "Rapid chromosomal evolution in island mice." ).

[edit]
I also wanted to say something about the morals debate. "Ethics" and "morals" are just concepts we've come up with to define all the unwritten rules in our society. They aren't some magical knowledge that's imbued into us, they are rules and guidelines the we follow in order to maintain a stable society. We receive our morals through our parents, our peers, TV, and any other contact with other people from our society. Different societies can have vastly different morals, it just depends on what you were told is "bad" as you grew up. Take incest, for example. It isn't taboo because it's somehow evil, it's taboo because inbreeding narrows gene pools and is more likely to produce damaged offspring.
[/edit]

On her radio show recently, Dr. Laura Schlesinger said that, as an observant
Orthodox Jew, homosexuality is an abomination according to Leviticus 18:22, and cannot be condoned under any circumstance. The following response is an open letter to Dr. Laura which was posted on the Internet. It's funny, as well as informative.

Dear Dr. Laura:

Thank you for doing so much to educate people regarding God's Law. I
have learned a great deal from your show, and try to share that
knowledge with as many people as I can. When someone tries to defend
the homosexual lifestyle, for example, I
simply remind them that Leviticus 18:22 clearly states it to be an abomination. End of debate.
I do need some advice from you, however, regarding some other elements
of God's Law and how to follow them.

1. When I burn a bull on the altar as a sacrifice, I know it creates a
pleasing odor for the Lord -Lev.1:9. The problem is my neighbors. They
claim the odor is not pleasing to them. Should I smite them?

2. I would like to sell my daughter into slavery, as sanctioned in
Exodus 21:7. In this day and age, what do you think would be a
fair price for her?

3. I know that I am allowed no contact with a woman while she is in
her period of menstrual uncleanliness- Lev.15: 19-24. The problem is how do I tell? I have tried asking, but most women take
offense.

4. Lev. 25:44 states that I may indeed possess slaves, both male and
female,
provided they are purchased from neighboring nations. A friend of mine claims that this applies to Mexicans, but not
Canadians. Can you clarify? Why can't I own Canadians?

5. I have a neighbor who insists on working on the Sabbath. Exodus 35:2.
The passage clearly states he should be put to death.
Am I morally obligated to kill him myself?

6. A friend of mine feels that even though eating shellfish is an
abomination - Lev. 11:10, it is a lesser abomination than
homosexuality. I don't agree. Can you settle this? Are there 'degrees'
of abomination?

7. Lev. 21:20 states that I may not approach the altar of God if I
have a defect in my sight. I have to admit that I wear reading
glasses. Does my vision have to be 20/20, or is there some wiggle room here?
8. Most of my male friends get their hair trimmed, including the hair
around their temples, even though this is expressly forbidden by Lev.19:27. How should they die.
9. I know from Lev. 11:6-8 that touching the skin of a dead pig makes me unclean, but may I still play football if I wear gloves?

10. My uncle has a farm. He violates Lev. 19:19 by planting two
different crops in the same field, as does his wife by wearing
garments made of two different kinds of thread (cotton/polyester
blend). He also tends to curse and blaspheme a lot. Is it
really necessary that we go to all the trouble of getting the whole town
together to stone them? - Lev.24:10-16. Couldn't
we just burn them to death at a private family affair like we do with
people who sleep with their in-laws? (Lev. 20:14)

I know you have studied these things extensively and thus enjoy
considerable expertise in such matters, so I am confident you can help.

Thank you again for reminding us that God's word is eternal and

unchanging.

Your adoring fan,

James M. Kauffman, Ed.D.

Professor Emeritus

University of Virginia

#102681 272 2005-09-22T03:11:14Z Atilla
Quote: macaddict, 2005-09-21T16:08:48Z
Christians do not claim to be infallable. Science does, so when they make significant mistakes, it crushes their credibility.

A true scientist will never, ever claim to be infallible. All scientific theories are stated to be the most accurate model for predicting the actions of a limited system and/or the most likely explanation based on observational evidence. That is why they are called THEORIES. That is why all scientific studies MUST make an allowance for errors, and all results MUST state these errors. And I've yet to hear a scientist go around saying that their theory is the one true theory, the one eternal truth, and anyone who disagrees can go suck. The BEST theory, perhaps, but that's where the line is generally drawn.


Quote: macaddict, 2005-09-21T16:08:48Z
On the flood: Did you know that scientists consider that Mars used to be completely covered in water? Now the surface is totally dry.  On the other hand, the Earth is covered with 75% water, and scientists calculate that, if all the water were to condensate at once, it would be enough to cover Mt. Everest and 2 miles higher. Or something like that.

That's because Mars is too small to hold a significant atmosphere, and has suffered from global cooling due to a lack of greenhouse effect. This occurred over millions of years. It's surface is now too cold for liquid water to exist. Earth was somewhat warmer in its early life (before life existed) due to the abudance of CO2, which would have meant higher sea levels. There is no evidence nor compelling reason for all Earth's water suddenly deciding to condense a few thousand years ago.

And why do you quote scientists after ranting about how inaccurate they are?

Quote: macaddict, 2005-09-21T16:08:48Z
That argument you've heard is stupid.  This one.

Quote: macaddict, 2005-09-22T03:08:56Z
And for the last half of #3, did you know that science owes almost all that it is now to Christianity?All the pagan beliefs before the Church age believed that the world was constantly chaos and subject to no rules because of the unsure behavior of the gods.  But Christianity started the idea that nature behaves according to rules, thus effectively inspiring science to begin again.

No it doesn't. Long before Christianity began, Greek philosophers founded the notion that the universe followed certain rules. They differed from scientist only in that they sought to determine such rules from reason alone, without reference to observational evidence. Many of those who instigated the rise from psuedo-science to true science were dabblers in arts that went against the Christian faith because they were deemed to be pagan rituals or unholy sorcery. Furthermore, many organisations, religious or otherwise, encouraged a strong sense of order and rigid laws. The Roman Empire was particularly notable for this. Buddhists believe in supernatural beings, but only in the same way they believe in people - they are a part of the world order, not some force outside it. The whole purpose of Confuscianism is to promote order and law. In Taoism, everything is believed to follow the grand universal harmony. In any event, whether science "owes" anything to Christianity is irrelevant. They are entirely different; one is a religion and the other a system for recording and analysing observations as objectively as possible.

Anyway... "proof" of evolution? Here's some of the best I can give, at the moment. Excuse the length.

Quote: ian johnston
The first step in demonstrating the truth of evolution is to make the claim that all living creatures must have a living parent. This point has been overwhelmingly established in the past century and a half, ever since the French scientist Louis Pasteur demonstrated how fermentation took place and thus laid to rest centuries of stories about beetles arising spontaneously out of dung or gut worms being miraculously produced from non-living material. There is absolutely no evidence for this ancient belief. Living creatures must come from other living creatures. It does no damage to this point to claim that life must have had some origin way back in time, perhaps in a chemical reaction of inorganic materials (in some primordial soup) or in some invasion from outer space. That may well be true. But what is clear is that any such origin for living things or living material must result in a very simple organism. There is no evidence whatsoever that inorganic chemical processes can produce complex, multi-cellular living creatures (the recent experiments cloning sheep, of course, are based on living tissue from other sheep).

The second important point in the case for evolution is that some living creatures are very different from some others. This, I take it, is self-evident. Let me cite a common example: many animals have what we call an internal skeletal structure featuring a backbone and skull. We call these animals vertebrates. Most animals do not have these features (we call them invertebrates). The distinction between vertebrates and invertebrates is something no one who cares to look at samples of both can reasonably deny, and, so far as I am aware, no one hostile to evolution has ever denied a fact so apparent to anyone who observes the world for a few moments.

The final point in the case for evolution is this: simple animals and plants existed on earth long before more complex ones (invertebrate animals, for example, were around for a very long time before there were any vertebrates). Here again, the evidence from fossils is overwhelming. In the deepest rock layers, there are no signs of life. The first fossil remains are of very simple living things. As the strata get more recent, the variety and complexity of life increase (although not at a uniform rate).   And no human fossils have ever been found except in the most superficial layers of the earth (e.g., battlefields, graveyards, flood deposits, and so on).  In all the countless geological excavations and inspections (for example, of the Grand Canyon), no one has ever come up with a genuine fossil remnant which goes against this general principle (and it would only take one genuine find to overturn this principle).

If we put these three points together, the rational case for evolution is air tight. If all living creatures must have a living parent, if living creatures are different, and if simpler forms were around before the more complex forms, then the more complex forms must have come from the simpler forms (e.g., vertebrates from invertebrates). There is simply no other way of dealing reasonably with the evidence we have. Of course, one might deny (as some do) that the layers of the earth represent a succession of very lengthy epochs and claim, for example, that the Grand Canyon was created in a matter of days, but this surely violates scientific observation and all known scientific processes as much as does the claim that, say, vertebrates just, well, appeared one day out of a spontaneous combination of chemicals.

And then, how do these new species form?
Geographical isolation- two segments get cut off.
Natural selection- to adapt to their environment, the weaker and less well adapted die and reproduce less than the others. Say, one group might get gradually smaller to hide in rocky gaps, and the other might delelop longer beacks to dig up insects. By develop, I mean that the longer beaked birds are ALWAYS more likely to get insects, so more ALWAYS survive. Thus, there beaks get gradually longer and the other birds gradually smaller. This would just be one adaption. And then there's random mutations (which are proven to exist, in humans too) to deal with, as well- if one actually ends up benefical, chances are that animal will reproduce, the reproduced animals will also reproduce with the beneficial mutation, untill eventually they all have the mutation. Make sense?
Eventually, they'll end up different species due to their adaptions.

#102802 284 2005-09-22T16:12:14Z Lucidium
You keep shouting that "no one has ever seen new genetic information arise". Do you even know what that means? Because it sounds like you're just parroting creationist slogans without understanding them.
[edit]Oh, and no one has ever seen the light sensitive molecules in our eyes react to light either, so we must all be blind.[/edit]

Genetic information is just a sequence of atoms in a DNA molecule. If the DNA molecule gets longer over a few generations, there's new information there. All that needs to happen is for more material to be added, it's not exactly impossible, or even unlikely.
Did anyone notice my post a couple pages back, with several examples of observed speciation, along with references?

I also said something about ethics in that post. Your obsession with an "unchanging" source for morals is just soppy. We have certain rules and taboos in society because it keeps our society stable. We have feelings of guilt because we were brought up being told certain things were wrong, and being punished when we were caught. You can look at any other society, and find people doing things that we'd think horrific with no feelings of guilt at all.
Yes, our society will change. And yes, our "ethics" will change along with it, just as they have done in the past. Pretending that there is some unchanging source for morals is, frankly, pretty naive. Nothing is black and white like that, and there will always be situations where your inflexible morals will break, or even force you to do something more evil than the thing your ethics prohibit.

Finally, this whole evidence thing. This is one of the most major differences between science and religion. You've got a preconceived conclusion (your particular religion), and you're picking and choosing evidence that backs it up. Science collects all the available evidence, and produces a conclusion that fits as best it can. Anyone can come up with an idea, and find evidence to back it up. But that doesn't make it any more valid than any other random theory. It's backwards, and that's not how science works.

The meaning of science is "to know". For something to become science, it can't just memely be thought or guessed at. We're pretty sure blackholes exist, but until they are entirely studied and proven, they are not science. Religion is not science. It can never be science. It can never be proven true.

So why believe in it? Faith.

If you have to run around trying to prove your religion is true (or is "scientific fact") to other people, then you have no faith. You don't believe that your own religion is true. You need, for yourself, proof. I'm not saying that religion is wrong. I just saying that religion, by its nature, ceases to exist if it becomes true. If religion becomes true, it is science and there is no reason for people not to believe it.

For example, we have no reason not to believe that the earth revolves around the sun. It is true. It has been proven with tests and experiments. Not believing that the earth revolves around the sun would be considered ignorance or a lapse in logic. Not believing there is a God would be a lack of faith. Faith. Get it?

If God came down to earth, held an assembly, and told everyone He was real, then would we have religion? No. Everyone would go, "Well, I guess God is real. Let's all add God to our list of facts and move on." Everyone would go to heaven. Religion would become just another element of science.

But that won't happen. Why? Because religion will always be religion. No one will ever be able to prove, no matter how often they post on a forum, that there is a God.

#102814 287 2005-09-22T17:08:04Z Lucidium
Fantastic post, blue. I agree with just about everything you said there. But I just can't let that bit about evolution slide. I know you were using it as an example, but this is a common misconception that I want to point out.

Nothing is ever really proven in science. We just have the theories that fit the evidence best, and provide the most useful predictions. The current theory of evolution happens to be the one that fits the evidence the best. This whole debate that's going on at the moment about creationism is making it seem a lot more contested than it really is. The media, and creationist campaigns, want to make it out to be this big debate. But in the scientific community, evolution is an accepted theory (in the scientific sense of the word), that finds use in many different areas, and provides useful predictions.

Oh, haven't you? Well, that's not the fault of anyone but yourself. When arguing, you need to concede the things that are brought against you that you can't discredit, as opposed to stating, "Well, I haven't seen it, so it can't possibly exist to bring my argument down!"

So, you're arguing that because Jesus didn't say insane things, that he has to be right? Fantastic argument.

The problem is that whether he rose from the dead or not. If he did, if it could be proven beyond a shadow of a doubt that Jesus absolutely and unconditionally rose from the grave, then yes, Christianity, and the teachings of Jesus, would be proven to be true, beyond the following:

Christianity makes an attempt to prove itself on merit of itself, and on merit of its own text. You can't, logically, do this. When asked why God exists, most theologians in support of Christianity point to the Bible, and the things written in the Bible, as the word of the God whose idea was introduced by the events... in the Bible. See the conundrum there? I'll make it a bit clearer:

I could write, on a piece of paper, right now, a paragraph outlining why I am God and Lord of everything in the universe. I'm obviously not, but when confronted if I exist or not, I point to the piece of paper. Since I'm God, and that is the word of God, I am indeed God. See the problem? This is what Christianity makes an attempt to do.

Quote: macaddict_17, 2005-09-22T15:17:44Z
A single cell is infinetly more complex than even an airport.  Simply the number of requirements that must be met for a single celled organism is just improbable. I claim that I can't prove heaven, and you say their probably isn't heaven.  Without evidence, I might add. Yet you say Evolution is proved and I say its totally improbable and back it up with evidence.

I gotta jump into this one, too. The burden of proof for heaven (And for evolution, might I add) is on the person who is trying to prove that it is there. Thus, neither strict evolutionists, nor strict creationists (Nor those trying to prove the existence of a personal deity, either) can claim with any certainty that any of those are correct. So, there doesn't need to be evidence to disprove heaven, and there doesn't need to be evidence to disprove evolution, since they are both theories, and both are still debated, and will be until they are proven.

The fact is that evolution is a theory, and it isn't proven. And it more than likely can't be proven. Same with the Big Bang, same with Christianity. None of them can actually and totally be proven. Hell, the entire basis of science and spacial laws is nothing but a theory. But so long as that theory explains succinctly what we see and feel around us, that then becomes our current ideal of 'truth.'

Quote: macaddict_17, 2005-09-22T15:17:44Z
You're not helping evolution there at all.  You look into the past and say 'Creation can't be proved because its not observable' Then you look again into the past and say 'Evolution must be true because we can look into the past and see it happening.'

True evolutionists never claim this. As I said, those who actually study and research evolution don't ever claim it to be true. That's a fallacy of those who argue it wrongly.

Quote: macaddict_17, 2005-09-22T15:17:44Z
It does tell things that happen to prove a point.  I think science shouldn't tell about the origins of life, because they can't observe it now.

Nor should religion, then. Science comes up with theories as to the origins of life. Religion just skips that part and says, "This is the way it was."

There's a big discrepancy between the two. As I said, Science doesn't claim to be the way, and the only way, at explaining things. Science claims that it could be wrong, and that the current accepted theory is that, and only that, wheras Religion claims to be the end-all and be-all.

Quote: macaddict, 2005-09-22T15:17:44Z

all scientific theories must be testable by experimentation and observation,

You're not helping evolution there at all. You look into the past and say 'Creation can't be proved because its not observable' Then you look again into the past and say 'Evolution must be true because we can look into the past and see it happening.'

I was not trying to help evolution. I was stating a fact. And only theologists say things like that. Scientists say "From the data we can collect, the Theory of Evolution appears to be the most likely explanation for the origin of species". Saying things MUST be true is a good way to get laughed at in the scientific community.

Quote: macaddict, 2005-09-22T15:17:44Z
NO ONE HAS EVER SEEN NEW GENETIC INFORMATION ARISE!!!! This is absolutely vital for 'Goo-to-you-via-the-Zoo evolution'.

Actually, we have. The DNA of rodent cells can be changed in experiments by exposing them to high levels of radiation (when trying to create a cancer, for instance). And take note of the stuff about hybrids above.

Quote: macaddict, 2005-09-22T15:17:44Z
A single cell is infinetly more complex than even an airport.  Simply the number of requirements that must be met for a single celled organism is just improbable.

The chance of molecules randomly coming together to form a single-celled organism is low. However, that is not how it happens. Smaller, self-replicating chemical reactions develop first, and later combine to form more complex cells. In fact, we can create such reactions in a lab by creating a mock 'primordial soup' (water, nitrogen, CO2, some other stuff) and passing an electric current through it, although I haven't heard of anyone letting them sit long enough for cells to form. I also point out that even if something is improbable, it's had billions of years to happen in, which raises the chances quite a bit.

Quote: macaddict, 2005-09-22T15:17:44Z
If you don't worship your creator, why should he do anything for you? He's doing us a favor simply by allowing us who repent to come to us, and you expect more?

Oooh, self-interested too. Sounds like a giant human with an ego problem, to me. "I'm so great, you're all privileged just to bask in my presence. Arrogant? No, of course not. I'm perfect. How could I be arrogant?"

Now watch macaddict get very, very upset.

Quote: macaddict, 2005-09-22T15:17:44Z
I think science shouldn't tell about the origins of life, because they can't observe it now.

Neither can priests, although I realise religion doesn't have the 'empirical evidence' requirement of science. In any event, this goes back to what I said above - science's version of the origion of life is an extrapolation based on the theory which seems most probable with the given evidence. Making predictions is what a scientific theory is supposed to be used for - nobody claims they're infallible, they're just considered the most likely on what evidence we have.
